\displaystyle 0=Z^{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\Xi^{{{{\bm{\upbeta}}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}+2Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}-Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upbeta}},

(2.18)

where
Z_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}:=\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}
and
\Xi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}:=\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upbeta}}
.

To understand
\mathbb{PT}
more fully, we realise it as a Kleinian geometry. Let us fix a pure spinor
\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
, and denote by
R
the stabiliser of its span in
G
. This is a parabolic subgroup of
G
. Then,
\mathbb{PT}
is isomorphic to
G/R
. One could equivalently realise
\mathbb{PT}
as the quotient of
\mathrm{SO}(n+2,\mathbb{C})
by the stabiliser of the corresponding
\gamma
-plane
\check{\Xi}
in
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{r}
of
R
induces a
|2|
-grading on
\mathfrak{g}
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{2}
, where
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{r}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{2}
, with
\mathfrak{r}_{0}\cong\mathfrak{gl}(m+1,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\cong\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\cong\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\cong(\mathfrak{r}_{1})^{*}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\cong(\mathfrak{r}_{2})^{*}
. In matrix form, this reads as

These
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
-modules satisfy the commutation relations
[\mathfrak{r}_{i},\mathfrak{r}_{j}]\subset\mathfrak{r}_{i+j}
where
\mathfrak{r}_{i}=\{0\}
for
|i|>2
. Further,
\mathfrak{g}
is equipped with a filtration of
\mathfrak{r}
-modules
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{r}^{-2}\supset\mathfrak{r}^{-1}\supset\mathfrak{r}^{0}\supset\mathfrak{r}^{1}\supset\mathfrak{r}^{2}
where
\mathfrak{r}^{i}:=\mathfrak{r}_{i}\oplus\mathfrak{r}^{i+1}
satisfy
[\mathfrak{r}^{i},\mathfrak{r}^{j}]\subset\mathfrak{r}^{i+j}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{r}
is not an irreducible
\mathfrak{r}
-module, but admits a splitting into irreducible
\mathfrak{r}
-submodules
\mathfrak{r}^{-1}/\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{-2}/\mathfrak{r}^{-1}
. Since the tangent space at any point of
G/R
can be identified with the quotient
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{r}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{T}(G/R)\cong G\times_{R}\left(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{r}\right)
, the tangent bundle of
\mathbb{PT}
admits a filtration of
R
-invariant subbundles
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{PT}=\mathrm{T}^{-2}\mathbb{PT}\supset\mathrm{T}^{-1}\mathbb{PT}
, where the rank-
(m+1)
distribution

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}^{-1}\mathbb{PT}:=G\times_{R}\big(\mathfrak{r}^{-1}/\mathfrak{r}\big)

(2.19)

is
maximally non-integrable
by virtue of the commutation relations among the various graded pieces of
\mathfrak{g}
, i.e., at every point
Z\in\mathbb{PT}
,
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}\cong\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
and
[\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{Z}\mathbb{PT},\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}]+\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}\cong\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{-2}
.

We shall presently show that the distributions
\mathrm{D}
defined in terms of (
2.17
) and
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\mathbb{PT}
defined by (
2.19
) are the same. We first note that any spinor
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\in\mathbb{S}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z_{(0)}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\sum_{k=1}^{[(m+1)/2]}\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\left(Z_{(-2k)}\cdot\Xi\right)^{\bm{\upalpha}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{Z^{\bm{\upalpha}} =}{}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{[(m+1)/2]}\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\left(Z_{(-2k-1)}\cdot\Xi\right)^{\bm{\upalpha}},

(2.20)

where
Z_{(-i)}\in\wedge^{i}\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\cong\wedge^{i}\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
, and
\left[\frac{m+1}{2}\right]
is
\frac{m+1}{2}
when
m+1
is even,
\frac{m}{2}
when
m+1
is odd. Here, the
\cdot
denotes the Clifford action, i.e.,
\left(\Phi\cdot\Xi\right)^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\Phi^{\mathcal{A}}\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upbeta}}
, and so on as extended to the action of
\wedge^{\bullet}\mathbb{V}
on
\mathbb{S}
. The factors have been chosen for convenience. The representation (
2.20
) is sometimes referred to as the
Fock representation

[
7
]
, and is already used implicitly in Cartan’s work
[
10
]
, where the
Z_{(-i)}
are viewed as the components of a spinor.

Now, using (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), together with (
2.14
) applied to
\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
, we compute
\Gamma^{{{(m+2)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z_{(0)}\Gamma^{{{(m+2)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
and, for
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{{{(m-2k+1)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k+1}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}Z^{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k}}}}_{{{(-2k)}}}\Gamma^{{{(m+1)}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k}\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k+1}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Gamma
^{{{(m-2k+1)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_1 \ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k+1}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}} Z^{\bm{\upalpha}} \Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}} =}{}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}Z^{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k+1}}}}_{{{(-2k-1)}}}\Gamma^{{{(m+2)}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k+1}\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k+1}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{{{(m-2k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}Z^{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k+1}}}}_{{{(-2k-1)}}}\Gamma^{{{(m+1)}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k+1}\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Gamma
^{{{(m-2k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_1 \ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}} Z^{\bm{\upalpha}} \Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}} =}{}+\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k+1}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}Z^{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k+2}}}}_{{{(-2k-2)}}}\Gamma^{{{(m+2)}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{2k+2}\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{m-2k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}.

(2.21)

Here, we have added tractor indices to the
Z_{(-i)}
. We can immediately conclude

Lemma 2.7
.

The conditions that
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]\in\mathbb{P}\mathbb{S}
lies in
\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
respectively are equivalent to

\displaystyle Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z_{(0)}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(Z_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}-\tfrac{1}{4}\big(Z_{(-2)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}},

(2.22)

\displaystyle Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z_{(0)}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(Z_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}},

(2.23)

respectively, up to overall factors. When
Z_{(0)}
is non-zero,
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
given by (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) lies in
\mathrm{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
and
\mathrm{D}_{\Xi}
respectively. In particular,
\mathrm{D}\cong\mathrm{T}^{-1}\mathbb{PT}
.

Proof.

Equations (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) follow from definitions (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) using (
2.21
). Equation (
2.23
) with
Z_{(0)}=1
coincides with the exponential of an element of
\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
and thus describes a point in
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
. The story for (
2.22
) is similar.
∎

On the other hand, using (
2.14
) or referring to
[
10
]
, the condition that
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
be pure is that

\displaystyle Z_{(0)}Z_{(-2k-1)}=Z_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-2k)},

\displaystyle Z_{(0)}Z_{(-2k)}=Z_{(-2)}\wedge Z_{(-2k+2)},\qquad k=1,\ldots,[(m+1)/2].

(2.24)

A dense open subset of
\mathbb{PT}
containing
[\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
can be obtained by intersecting the locus (
2.24
) with the affine subspace
Z_{(0)}=1
in
\mathbb{S}
. Summarising,

Proposition 2.8
.

The twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
of a
(2m+1)
-dimensional smooth quadric
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
has dimension
\frac{1}{2}(m+1)(m+2)
, and is equipped with a maximally non-integrable distribution
\mathrm{D}
of rank
m+1
, i.e.,
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{PT}=\mathrm{D}+[\mathrm{D},\mathrm{D}]
, where, for any
\Xi\in\mathbb{PT}
,
\mathrm{D}_{\Xi}
is a dense open subset of
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
as defined by (
2.17
).

Further, for any
\Xi\in\mathbb{PT}
, the projective tangent space
\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
intersects
\mathbb{PT}
in a
(2m+1)
-dimensional linear subspace of
\mathbb{PT}
, and
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
is an
(m+1)
-dimensional linear subspace of
\mathbb{PT}
.

Proof.

The first part has already been explained and stems from the general theory of
[
9
]
.

For the second part, we fix a pure spinor
\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
, and let
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
be an element of the projective tangent space
\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
so that
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
takes the form (
2.22
). If
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
also lies in
\mathbb{PT}
, then, with reference to (
2.24
),
Z_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0
and
Z_{(-2)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0
. Generically,
Z_{(-1)}
is non-zero, so
Z_{(-2)}=Z_{(-1)}\wedge\Phi_{(-1)}
for some
\Phi_{(-1)}\in\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
. The form of
Z_{(-2)}
remains invariant under the transformation
\Phi_{(-1)}\mapsto\Phi_{(-1)}+aZ_{(-1)}
for any
a\in\mathbb{C}
. The choice of
Z_{(0)}
is cancelled out by the freedom in the choice of scale of (
2.22
). Thus,
\dim\left(\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}\cap\mathbb{PT}\right)=2\times(m+1)-1=2m+1
. If
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
lies in
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
, then it takes the form (
2.23
). In this case, the purity conditions (
2.24
) do not yield any further constraints, and thus
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
must also lie in
\mathbb{PT}
.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

The rank-
(m+1)
distribution
\mathrm{D}
will be referred to as the
canonical distribution
of
\mathbb{PT}
.

When
m=1
, the twistor space of
\mathcal{Q}^{3}
is simply
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
and the canonical distribution
{\mathrm{D}}
is the rank-
2
contact distribution annihilated by the contact
1
-form
\bm{\alpha}:=\Gamma^{(0)}_{\bm{\upalpha}\bm{\upbeta}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\mathrm{d}Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}
.
The appropriate generalisation of this contact
1
-form to dimension
2m+1
is then the set of
1
-forms

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}^{\bm{\upalpha}\bm{\upbeta}}:=Z^{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\mathrm{d}Z^{{{{\bm{\upbeta}}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}+2Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathrm{d}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}-Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}\mathrm{d}Z^{\bm{\upbeta}},

(2.25)

annihilating the canonical distribution
{\mathrm{D}}
. Here, the homogeneous coordinates
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
are assumed to satisfy (
2.14
) or (
2.15
).

The following lemma follows directly from the exponential map from a given complement of
\mathfrak{r}
in
\mathfrak{g}
to a dense open subset of
\mathbb{PT}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\Xi
be a point in
\mathbb{PT}
, and let
\mathfrak{r}
be its stabiliser in
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
is foliated by a family of distinguished curves passing through
\Xi
parametrised by the points of the
(m+1)
-dimensional module
\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
, for any decomposition
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{2}
.

Geometric correspondences.
The bilinear forms (
2.2
) can also be used to characterise the intersections of
\gamma
-planes in terms of their corresponding pure spinors.

Theorem 2.11

(
[
10
,
17
]
)
.

Let
Z
and
W
be two twistors with homogeneous coordinates
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
and
[W^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
, and corresponding
\gamma
-planes
\check{Z}
and
\check{W}
in
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
respectively. Then

\displaystyle\dim\big(\check{Z}\cap\check{W}\big)\geq k\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Gamma^{{{(\ell)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0,\qquad\mbox{for all}\quad\ell\leq k.

Further,
\dim(\check{Z}\cap\check{W})=k
if and only if in addition
\Gamma^{{{(k+1)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}\neq 0
.

A direct application leads to

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\Xi
and
Z
be two twistors with corresponding
\gamma
-planes
\check{\Xi}
and
\check{Z}
respectively. Then

1.

\dim(\check{\Xi}\cap\check{Z})\geq m-3
if and only if there exists
W\in\mathbb{PT}
such that
W\in\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}\cap\mathbf{T}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}
or
W\in\mathbf{D}_{Z}\cap\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
.

2.

\dim(\check{\Xi}\cap\check{Z})\geq m-2
if and only if
\Xi\in\mathbf{T}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}
if and only if
Z\in\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
if and only if there exists
W\in\mathbb{PT}
such that
Z,\Xi\in\mathbf{D}_{W}
, or equivalenly
W\in\mathbf{D}_{Z}\cap\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
.

3.

\dim(\check{\Xi}\cap\check{Z})\geq m-1
if and only if
Z\in\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
if and only if
\Xi\in\mathbf{D}_{Z}
.

Proof.

We fix
\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
and we assume that
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
is given by (
2.20
) with components
Z_{(-i)}
satisfying (
2.24
). In each case, we apply Theorem
2.11
and compute
\Gamma^{{{(\ell)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0
to derive conditions on
Z_{(-i)}
. With no loss of generality, we may assume
Z_{(0)}=1
.

1. We have
Z_{(-i)}
for all
i\geq 4
and
Z_{(-2)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0
, i.e.,
Z_{(-2)}=\Phi_{(-1)}\wedge\Psi_{(-1)}
for some
\Phi_{(-1)},\Psi_{(-1)}\in\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
, and
Z_{(-3)}=Z_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=Z_{(-1)}\wedge\Phi_{(-1)}\wedge\Psi_{(-1)}
. A suitable
W\in\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}\cap\mathbf{T}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}
is given by
W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(Z_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi)^{\bm{\upalpha}}
and
W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\frac{1}{4}((\Phi_{(-1)}\wedge\Psi_{(-1)})\cdot Z)^{\bm{\upalpha}}
, and similarly for a suitable
W\in\mathbf{D}_{Z}\cap\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
.

2. The first two equivalences follow immediately from Proposition
2.8
and Theorem
2.11
. For the last equivalence, we have
Z_{(-i)}
for all
i\geq 3
, so that
Z_{(-2)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0
and
Z_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0
, i.e.,
Z_{(-2)}=Z_{(-1)}\wedge\Phi_{(-1)}
for some
\Phi_{(-1)}\in\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
. A suitable
W\in\mathbf{D}_{Z}\cap\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
is given by
W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(Z_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi)^{\bm{\upalpha}}
and
W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(\Phi_{(-1)}\cdot Z)^{\bm{\upalpha}}
.

3. This follows immediately from Proposition
2.8
and Theorem
2.11
.
∎

In a similar vein, we obtain

Proposition 2.13
.

Fix a twistor
\Xi
in
\mathbb{PT}
and let
\check{\Xi}
be its corresponding
\gamma
-plane in
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
. Let
Z
and
W
be two twistors in
\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
, corresponding to
\gamma
-planes
\check{Z}
and
\check{W}
. Then
\dim(\check{Z}\cap\check{W})\geq m-4
.

Further, if
Z
and
W
take the respective forms

\displaystyle Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z_{(0)}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(Z_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}-\tfrac{1}{4}\big(Z_{(-2)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}},

\displaystyle W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=W_{(0)}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(W_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}-\tfrac{1}{4}\big(W_{(-2)}\cdot\Xi\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}},

where
Z_{(0)}Z_{(-2)}=Z_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-1)}
,
Z_{(-2)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0
,
W_{(0)}W_{(-2)}=W_{(-1)}\wedge W_{(-1)}
and
W_{(-2)}\wedge W_{(-2)}=0
, then

\displaystyle\dim\big(\check{Z}\cap\check{W}\big)\geq m-3\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle Z_{(-2)}\wedge W_{(-2)}=0,

(2.26a)

\displaystyle\dim\big(\check{Z}\cap\check{W}\big)\geq m-2\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle Z_{(-1)}\wedge W_{(-2)}+W_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-2)}=0,

(2.26b)

\displaystyle\dim\big(\check{Z}\cap\check{W}\big)\geq m-1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle W_{(-2)}-Z_{(-2)}-W_{(-1)}\wedge Z_{(-1)}=0.

(2.26c)

Proof.

Let us rewrite

\displaystyle W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(\Phi_{(-1)}\cdot Z\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}-\tfrac{1}{4}\big(\Phi_{(-2)}\cdot Z\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{W^{\bm{\upalpha}} =}{}-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{8}\big(\big(\Phi_{(-1)}\wedge\Phi_{(-2)}\big)\cdot Z\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\tfrac{1}{32}\big(\big(\Phi_{(-2)}\wedge\Phi_{(-2)}\big)\cdot Z\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}},

where
\Phi_{-1}:=W_{-1}-Z_{-1}
and
\Phi_{-2}:=W_{-2}-Z_{-2}-W_{-1}\wedge Z_{-1}
. It suffices to compute
\Gamma^{{{(m-k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0
for all
k\geq 4
, and

1)

\Gamma^{{{(m-k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0
for all
k\geq 3
if and only if
\Phi_{(-2)}\wedge\Phi_{(-2)}=0
;

2)

\Gamma^{{{(m-k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0
for all
k\geq 2
if and only if
\Phi_{(-1)}\wedge\Phi_{(-2)}=0
;

3)

\Gamma^{{{(m-k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0
for all
k\geq 1
if and only if
\Phi_{(-2)}=0
.

Equivalences (
2.26a
), (
2.26b
) and (
2.26c
) now follow from the definitions of
\Phi_{(-1)}
and
\Phi_{(-2)}
.
∎

A special case of this proposition is given below.

Corollary 2.14
.

Fix a twistor
\Xi
in
\mathbb{PT}
and let
\check{\Xi}
be its corresponding
\gamma
-plane in
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
. Let
Z
and
W
be two twistors in
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
, corresponding to
\gamma
-planes
\check{Z}
and
\check{W}
. Then
\dim(\check{Z}\cap\check{W})\geq m-2
.

Further,
Z
and
W
belong to the same distinguished curve in
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
, as defined in Lemma
2.10
, if and only if
\dim(\check{Z}\cap\check{W})\geq m-1
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.13
with
Z_{(-2)}=W_{(-2)}=0
, and Lemma
2.10
.
∎

2.2.2
Even dimensions

Assume
n=2m
. Any non-zero chiral spinor
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
defines a linear map
Z_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}:=\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.04625pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.04625pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59703pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.70145pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}\colon\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
, and similarly for primed spinors. Again, any non-trivial kernel of this map descends to a linear subspace of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
. A non-zero chiral spinor
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
is
pure
if the kernel of
Z_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}
has maximal dimension
m+1
, and similarly for primed spinors.

Proposition 2.15

(
[
10
]
)
.

The twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
and the primed twistor space
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m}
are isomorphic to the projectivations of the spaces of all pure spinors in
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
respectively.

When
m=2
, all spinors in
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
are pure. When
m>2
, the analogue of the purity condition (
2.14
) is now
[
10
]

\displaystyle\Gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha}\bm{\upbeta}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad k<m+1,\quad k\equiv m+1\pmod{4},

(2.27)

or alternatively,
[
20
,
34
]
,
Z^{\mathcal{A}\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}Z_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}=0
. Again, we will think of
\mathbb{PT}
and
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}
as complex projective varieties of
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
respectively, when
m>2
, while for
\mathcal{Q}^{4}
, we have
\mathbb{PT}_{(4)}\cong\mathbb{CP}^{3}
.

The Kleinian model is again a homogeneous space
G/R
, where
R
is parabolic. But its parabolic Lie algebra
\mathfrak{r}
this time induces a
|1|
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{1}
on
\mathfrak{g}
, where
\mathfrak{r}_{0}\cong\mathfrak{gl}(m+1,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\cong\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{1}\cong\wedge^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{m+1})^{*}
, and
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{r}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{1}
, as given in matrix form by

Again, the one-dimensional center of
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
is spanned by a unique grading element with eigenvalues
i
on
\mathfrak{r}_{i}
. In this case, the tangent space of any point of
G/R
is irreducible and linearly isomorphic to
\mathfrak{r}_{-1}
.

Unlike in odd dimensions, the twistor space of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m}
is not equipped with any canonical
rank-
m
distribution. As we shall see in Section
2.2.3
, one requires an additional structure to endow
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m)}
with one.

Proposition 2.16
.

The twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
of a
2m
-dimensional smooth quadric
\mathcal{Q}^{2m}
has dimension
\frac{1}{2}m(m+1)
. Further, for any
Z
of
\mathbb{PT}
, the projective tangent space
\mathbf{T}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}
intersects
\mathbb{PT}
in a
(2m-1)
-dimensional linear subspace of
\mathbb{PT}
.

Arguments similar to those used in odd dimensions lead to the following proposition.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
Z
and
W
be two twistors corresponding to
\alpha
-planes
\check{Z}
and
\check{W}
. Then
\dim(\check{Z}\cap\check{W})\in\{m-2,m\}
if and only if
Z\in\mathbf{T}_{W}\mathbb{PT}
, or equivalently,
W\in\mathbf{T}_{Z}\mathbb{PT}
. Further, if
Z
and
W
lie in
\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
for some twistor
\Xi
in
\mathbb{PT}
, then
\dim(\check{Z}\cap\check{W})\in\{m-4,m-2,m\}
.

2.2.3
From even to odd dimensions

We note that as
\frac{1}{2}(m+1)(m+2)
-dimensional projective complex varieties of
\mathbb{CP}^{2^{m+1}-1}
, the respective twistor spaces
\mathbb{PT}:=\mathbb{PT}_{(2m+1)}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}:=\mathbb{PT}_{(2m+2)}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
are isomorphic. The only geometric structure that distinguishes the former from the latter is the rank-
(m+1)
canonical distribution. It is shown in
[
13
]
how
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
can be viewed as a ‘Fefferman space’ over
\mathbb{PT}
– in fact, this reference deals with a more general, curved, setting. Here, we explain how the canonical distribution on
\mathbb{PT}
arises as one ‘descends’ from
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
to
\mathbb{PT}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
be a
(2m+4)
-dimensional oriented complex vector space equipped with a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form
\tilde{h}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}
. Denote by
X^{\mathcal{A}}
the standard coordinates on
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
. As before, we realise
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
as a smooth quadric of
\mathbb{P}\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
with twistor spaces
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}^{\prime}
induced from the irreducible spinor representations
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
of
(\widetilde{\mathbb{V}},\tilde{h}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
. Now, fix a unit vector
U^{\mathcal{A}}
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
, so that
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}=\mathbb{U}\oplus\mathbb{V}
, where
\mathbb{U}:=\langle U^{\mathcal{A}}\rangle
, and
\mathbb{V}:=\mathbb{U}^{\perp}
is its orthogonal complement in
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
. Then
\mathbb{V}
is equipped with a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form
h_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}:=\tilde{h}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}-U_{\mathcal{A}}U_{\mathcal{B}}
, and we can realise
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
as a smooth quadric of
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{V}
with twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
induced from the irreducible spinor representation
\mathbb{S}
of
(\mathbb{V},h_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
.

Observe that
U^{\mathcal{A}}
defines two invertible linear maps,

\displaystyle U_{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}^{\bm{\upbeta}}:=U^{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.94907pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.94907pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.27203pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.32645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\colon\ \widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\qquad U_{\bm{\upalpha}}^{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}:=U^{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.10158pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.10158pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.80954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.86395pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}\bm{\upalpha}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.3819pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.3819pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.23405pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.44218pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\colon\ \widetilde{\mathbb{S}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime},

where
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.94907pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.94907pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.27203pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.32645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.10158pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.10158pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.80954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.86395pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}\bm{\upalpha}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.3819pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.3819pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.23405pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.44218pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
generate the Clifford algebra
{\mathcal{C}\ell}(\widetilde{\mathbb{V}},\tilde{h}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
. These maps allow us to identify
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}
with
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
, and thus
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
with
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}^{\prime}
. Further, using the Clifford property, it is straightforward to check that
\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.10158pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.10158pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.80954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.86395pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}\bm{\upalpha}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}:=h_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{B}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78906pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78906pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.60538pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71812pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upgamma^{\prime}}}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}\bm{\upalpha}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.99936pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.27455pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}U_{\bm{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{\bm{\upbeta}}=-h_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{B}}U_{\bm{\upalpha}}^{\bm{\upgamma^{\prime}}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.6383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.6383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.4702pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.75372pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}\bm{\upgamma^{\prime}}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=U^{\mathcal{B}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[16.69188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[16.69188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.28038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.34312pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\bm{\upalpha}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
generate the Clifford algebra
{\mathcal{C}\ell}(\mathbb{V},h_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
. More generally, the relation between the spanning elements of
{\mathcal{C}\ell}(\mathbb{V},h_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
and those of
{\mathcal{C}\ell}(\widetilde{\mathbb{V}},\widetilde{h}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}=h_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\cdots h_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}^{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{B}_{k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}},\qquad k\equiv m+2\pmod{2},

(2.28)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_{1}\ldots\mathcal{A}_{k}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}=U^{\mathcal{B}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{k}\mathcal{B}}{\bm{\upalpha\upbeta}}}}=(-1)^{k}h_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\cdots h_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}^{\mathcal{B}_{k}}U_{\bm{\upalpha}}^{\bm{\upgamma^{\prime}}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathcal{B}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{B}_{k}}{\bm{\upgamma^{\prime}\upbeta}}}},\qquad k\equiv m+1\pmod{2}.

If we now introduce homogeneous coordinates
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
on
\mathbb{P}\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}
, we can identify the twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
equipped with its canonical distribution with the twistor space
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
, as can be seen by inspection of (
2.14
) and (
2.27
). Note that we could have played the same game with
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}^{\prime}
.

Let us interpret this more geometrically. Clearly, the embedding of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
into
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
arises as the intersection of the hyperplane
U_{\mathcal{A}}X^{\mathcal{A}}=0
in
\mathbb{P}\widetilde{\mathbb{V}}
with the cone over
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
. A
\gamma
-plane of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
then arises as the intersection of an
\alpha
-plane of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
with
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
, and similarly for
\beta
-planes. An
\alpha
-plane
\check{Z}
and a
\beta
-plane
\check{W}
define the same
\gamma
-plane if and only if their corresponding twistors satisfy
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=U_{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}
. In particular, such a pair must intersect maximally, i.e., in an
m
-plane in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
. This much is already outlined in the appendix of
[
31
]
.

Finally, we can see how the canonical distribution
\mathrm{D}
on
\mathbb{PT}
arises geometrically from
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}^{\prime}
. Fix a point
[\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}
. This represents an
\alpha
-plane
\check{\Xi}
in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+2}
, and so a
\gamma
-plane in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
, which also corresponds to the unique
\beta
-plane with associated primed twistor
[U_{\bm{\upbeta}}^{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upbeta}}]
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}^{\prime}
. We claim that the
\beta
-planes intersecting
\check{\Xi}
maximally are in one-to-one correspondence with the points of
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
. To see this, let
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
be a point in
\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}\subset\mathbb{P}\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}
so that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}Z^{{{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\Xi^{{{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad k<m,\quad k\equiv m\pmod{2}.

We can then conclude
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]\in\mathbf{T}_{\Xi}\mathbb{PT}
by virtue of (
2.16
) and (
2.28
) as expected. Now, consider the set of all
\beta
-planes intersecting
\check{\Xi}
maximally: these correspond to all primed twistors
[W^{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}]\in\widetilde{\mathbb{PT}}^{\prime}
satisfying

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}W^{{{\bm{\upalpha^{\prime}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.04625pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.04625pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59703pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.70145pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\Xi^{{{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad k<m+1,\quad k\equiv m+1\pmod{2}.

Identifying
\beta
-planes and
\alpha
-planes on
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
, i.e., setting
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=U_{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}W^{\bm{\upbeta^{\prime}}}
, and using (
2.28
) again precisely yield that
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]\in\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}
by virtue of (
2.17
).

2.3
Correspondence space

We now formalise the correspondence between
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
and
\mathbb{PT}
.

2.3.1
Odd dimensions

Assume
n=2m+1
.

Definition 2.18
.

The
correspondence space
\mathbb{F}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
and
\mathbb{PT}
is the projective complex subvariety of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}\times\mathbb{PT}
defined as the set of points
([X^{\mathcal{A}}],[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}])
satisfying
the incidence relation

\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{A}}Z^{{{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}=0,

(2.29)

where
Z^{{{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}:=\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upbeta}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upalpha}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
.

The usual way of understanding the twistor correspondence is by means of the double fibration

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}^{n}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{PT},}

where
\mu
and
\nu
denote the usual projections of maximal rank.

Clearly, since, by definition, a twistor
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]
in
\mathbb{PT}
corresponds to a
\gamma
-plane of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
, namely the set of points
[X^{\mathcal{A}}]
in
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
satisfying (
2.29
), we see that each fiber of
\mu
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{CP}^{m}
.

Now, a point
x
of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
is sent to a compact complex submanifold
\hat{x}
of
\mathbb{PT}
isomorphic to the fiber
\mathbb{F}_{x}
of
\mathbb{F}
over
x
, and similarly, a subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
will correspond to a subset
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
of
\mathbb{PT}
swept out by those complex submanifolds
\{\hat{x}\}
parametrised by the points
x\in\mathcal{U}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{Q}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{x}:=\nu^{-1}(x)\mapsto\hat{x}:=\mu(\mathbb{F}_{x}),\qquad\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{U}}:=\!\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{U}}\!\nu^{-1}(x)\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{U}}:=\!\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{U}}\!\mu(\mathbb{F}_{x}).

To describe
\hat{x}
, it is enough to describe the fiber
\mathbb{F}_{x}
. By definition, this is the set of all
\gamma
-planes incident on
x
. If
\check{Z}
is a
\gamma
-plane incident on
x
, the intersection
\check{Z}\cap\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{Q}^{n}
is an
m
-dimensional subspace totally null with respect to the bilinear form on
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{Q}^{n}\cong\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
, which we shall also refer to a
\gamma
-plane. This descends to a
\gamma
-plane in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m-1}
viewed as the projectivisation of the null cone through
x
. Thus,
\hat{x}\cong\mathbb{F}_{x}
is isomorphic to the
\frac{1}{2}m(m+1)
-dimensional twistor space
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m-1}
.

We can get a little more information about
\mathbb{F}
by viewing it as the homogeneous space
G/Q
where
Q:=P\cap R
is the intersection of
P
, the stabiliser of a null line in
\mathbb{V}
, and
R
the stabiliser of a totally null
(m+1)
-plane containing that line. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{q}
of
Q
induces a
|3|
-grading on
\mathfrak{g}
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{q}_{-3}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{3}
, where
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{3}
. For convenience, we split
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 2}
further as
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}=\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}^{E}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}^{F}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 2}=\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 2}^{E}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 2}^{F}
. Also,
\mathfrak{q}_{0}\cong\mathfrak{gl}(m,\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}\cong\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{m})^{*}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{E}\cong\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}\cong\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{-3}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{m})^{*}
with
(\mathfrak{q}_{i})^{*}\cong\mathfrak{q}_{-i}
. In matrix form,
\mathfrak{g}
reads as

These modules satisfy the commutation relations
[\mathfrak{q}_{i},\mathfrak{q}_{j}]\subset\mathfrak{q}_{i+j}
where
\mathfrak{q}_{i}=\{0\}
for
|i|>3
. More precisely, the action of
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
on these modules, carefully distinguishing
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{E}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{F}
, can be recorded in the form of a diagram:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{F}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{-3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{F}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{F}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{r}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the dotted arrows give the relations between
\mathfrak{q}_{0}
-modules, and
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
- and
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
-modules. Invariance follows from the inclusions
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{E}\subset\mathfrak{r}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{F}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{E}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{F}\subset\mathfrak{r}_{1}
.

Beside the filtration of vector subbundles of
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{F}
determined by the grading on
\mathfrak{g}
, we distinguish three
Q
-invariant distributions of interest on
\mathbb{F}
:

•

the rank-
\frac{1}{2}m(m+1)
distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-2}_{F}\mathbb{F}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}
. It is integrable and tangent to the fibers of
\nu:G/Q\rightarrow G/P
, each isomorphic to the homogeneous space
P/Q
. This follows from the relations
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F},\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}]\subset\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}
,
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F},\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}]=0
, and
[\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F},\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}]=0
, and the fact that the kernel of the projection
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{q}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p}
is precisely
\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}\cong\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{q}
. In fact, since
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F},\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}]\subset\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}
, each fiber is itself equipped with the canonical distribution on
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
.

•

the rank-
m
distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{E}\mathbb{F}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}
. It is integrable and tangent to the fibers of
\mu:G/Q\rightarrow G/R
, each isomorphic to the homogeneous space
R/Q
. This follows from the relations
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E},\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}]=0
and the fact that the kernel of the projection
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{q}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{r}
is precisely
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}\cong\mathfrak{r}/\mathfrak{q}
.

•

the rank-
(2m+1)
distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-2}_{E}\mathbb{F}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{E}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}
. It is non-integrable and bracket generates
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{F}
since we have
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E},\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}]\subset\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{E}
,
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E},\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{E}]=0
,
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E},\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{F}]\subset\mathfrak{q}_{-3}
,
[\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F},\mathfrak{q}_{-2}^{E}]\subset\mathfrak{q}_{-3}
. Further, the quotient
\mathrm{T}^{-2}_{E}\mathbb{F}/\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{E}\mathbb{F}
descends to the canonical distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\mathbb{PT}
.

The twistor space and correspondence space of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
.

At this stage, we introduce a splitting (
2.3
) of
\mathbb{V}
, and as before denote by
\mathring{X}^{\mathcal{A}}
,
\mathring{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathring{Z}^{\mathcal{A}}_{a}
vectors in
\mathbb{V}_{1}
,
\mathbb{V}_{-1}
and
\mathbb{V}_{0}
respectively. There is an induced splitting (
2.8
) of
\mathbb{S}
, and we shall accordingly split the homogeneous twistor coordinates as
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=(\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}})
, or, using the injectors, as

\displaystyle Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\mathring{I}^{\bm{\upalpha}}_{\mathbf{A}}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}+\mathring{O}^{\bm{\upalpha}}_{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}.

(2.30)

Needless to say that Cartan’s theory of spinors applies to
\mathbb{S}_{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\mathbb{S}_{\frac{1}{2}}
in the obvious way and notation, as we have done in Section
2.2
. In particular, a spinor
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
is pure if and only if the kernel of the map
\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}:=\pi^{\mathbf{B}}\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00844pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.86317pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{{a}{\mathbf{B}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
is of maximal dimension
m
, and so on. The purity condition on
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
can then be re-expressed as follows.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=(\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}})
be a non-zero spinor in
\mathbb{S}\cong\mathbb{S}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{\frac{1}{2}}
. Then
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
is pure, i.e., satisfies (
2.14
), if and only if
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
and
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathbf{AB}}}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad k<m,\quad k\equiv m+1,m\pmod{4},

(2.31a)

\displaystyle\gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathbf{AB}}}}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\omega^{\mathbf{B}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad k<m,\quad k\equiv m+1,m\pmod{4},

(2.31b)

\displaystyle\gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathbf{AB}}}}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}=0,\qquad\text{for all}\quad k<m-1.

(2.31c)

Proof.

This is a direct computation using (
2.15
), (
2.9
) and (
2.30
). Writing
\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}:=\pi^{\mathbf{B}}\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00844pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.86317pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{{a}{\mathbf{B}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
and
\omega_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}:=\omega^{\mathbf{B}}\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00844pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.86317pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{{a}{\mathbf{B}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
, we find

\displaystyle\pi^{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\pi^{{{\mathbf{B}}}}_{{{a}}}+\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}=0,\qquad\omega^{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\omega^{{{\mathbf{B}}}}_{{{a}}}+\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\omega^{\mathbf{B}}=0,\qquad\pi^{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\omega^{{{\mathbf{B}}}}_{{{a}}}-\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\omega^{\mathbf{B}}+2\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}=0,

which are equivalent to (
2.31a
), (
2.31b
) and (
2.31c
) respectively
[
35
]
.
∎

By Cartan’s theory of spinors, condition (
2.31a
) is equivalent to
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
being pure provided it is non-zero, and similarly for condition (
2.31b
) and
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
. Condition (
2.31c
) is equivalent to the
\gamma
-planes of
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
and
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
intersecting in an
m
- or
(m-1)
-plane in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
provided these are non-zero.

Remark 2.20
.

The annihilator (
2.25
) of the canonical distribution of
\mathbb{PT}
can be re-expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bm{\alpha}^{\mathbf{AB}}_{(\omega,\omega)}:=\omega^{a\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{a}^{\mathbf{B}}+2\omega^{\mathbf{B}}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}-\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\mathbf{B}},\\
&\bm{\alpha}^{\mathbf{AB}}_{(\pi,\pi)}:=\pi^{a\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{B}}+2\pi^{\mathbf{B}}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}-\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathbf{B}},\\
&\bm{\alpha}^{\mathbf{AB}}_{(\omega,\pi)}:=\omega^{a\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{B}}+\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}+4\pi^{[\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\mathbf{B}]},\\
&\bm{\alpha}^{\mathbf{AB}}_{(\pi,\omega)}:=\pi^{a\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{a}^{\mathbf{B}}+\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\mathbf{B}}+4\omega^{[\mathbf{A}}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathbf{B}]},\end{split}

(2.32)

where we have used (
2.30
) and (
2.9
), and it is understood that
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
and
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
satisfy (
2.31
).

The twistor correspondence associates to the point
\infty
in
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
, with coordinates
[\mathring{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}]
, a complex submanifold
\widehat{\infty}
of
\mathbb{PT}
defined by the locus
\mathring{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}Z_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}=0
in
\mathbb{PT}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\infty\in\mathcal{Q}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{\infty}:=\nu^{-1}(\infty)\mapsto\widehat{\infty}:=\mu(\mathbb{F}_{\infty})=\mu\circ\nu^{-1}(\infty).

Points of
\widehat{\infty}
are parametrised by
[\omega^{\mathbf{A}},0]
. Since removing
\infty
from
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
yields complex Euclidean space
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
, we accordingly remove
\widehat{\infty}
to obtain the twistor space
\mathbb{PT}\setminus\{\widehat{\infty}\}=\mu\circ\nu^{-1}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n})
of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
. This will be denoted by
\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}
. This region of twistor space is parametrised by
\{[\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]\colon

\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\neq 0\}
.

The correspondence space of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
will be denoted
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}}
, and is parametrised by the coordinates
(x^{a},[\pi^{\mathbf{A}}])
, where
\{x^{a}\}
are the flat standard coordinates on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
and
[\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
are homogeneous pure spinor coordinates on the fibers of
\mathbb{F}
. These parametrise the
\gamma
-planes of the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
at a point
x
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
, and are related to
[\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{B}}]
by means of the incidence relation (
2.29
)

\displaystyle\omega^{\mathbf{A}}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}},

(2.33)

which can be obtained from (
2.7
), (
2.9
) and (
2.30
). Indeed, the
\gamma
-plane defined by
[\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
through the origin is given by the locus
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
, so that the
\gamma
-plane defined by
[\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
through any other point
\mathring{x}^{a}
is given by (
2.33
) with
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathring{x}^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
.

Remark 2.21
.

By (
2.33
) and (
2.31a
), for a holomorphic function
f
on
\mathbb{F}
to descend to
\mathbb{PT}
, it must be annihilated by the differential operator
\pi^{[\mathbf{A}}\pi^{{{a}{\mathbf{B}]}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.66249pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.66249pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.36955pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83539pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\nabla_{a}
.

2.3.2
Even dimensions

The double fibration picture in dimension
n=2m
is very similar to the odd-dimensional case, and we only summarise the discussion here.

We realise
\mathbb{F}
as a homogeneous space
G/Q
. Here, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{q}
of
Q
induces a
|2|
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{q}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{2}
on
\mathfrak{g}
, where
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{2}
. We split
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}
further as
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}=\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}^{E}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{\pm 1}^{F}
, and we have
\mathfrak{q}_{0}\cong\mathfrak{gl}(m,\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}\cong\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}\cong\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{-2}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{m})^{*}
with
(\mathfrak{q}_{i})^{*}\cong\mathfrak{q}_{-i}
. The action of
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
on these
\mathfrak{q}_{0}
-modules is recorded below together with the matrix form of the splitting:

The modules
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{F}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{-1}^{E}
give rise to two integrable
Q
-invariant distributions
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{F}\mathbb{F}
and
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{E}\mathbb{F}
on
\mathbb{F}
of rank
\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)
and
m
respectively, and tangent to the fibers of
G/Q\rightarrow G/P
and
G/Q\rightarrow G/R
respectively.

The twistor space and correspondence space of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m}
.

The even-dimensional analogue of Lemma
2.19
is recorded below.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=(\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}})
be a spinor in
\mathbb{S}\cong\mathbb{S}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}
. Then
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}
is pure if and only if
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
and
\pi^{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathbf{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}}}\pi^{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}}\pi^{\mathbf{B^{\prime}}}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all}\quad k<m,\quad k\equiv m\pmod{4},

(2.34a)

\displaystyle\gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathbf{AB}}}}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\omega^{\mathbf{B}}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all}\quad k<m,\quad k\equiv m\pmod{4},

(2.34b)

\displaystyle\gamma^{{{(k)}}}_{{{\mathbf{AB^{\prime}}}}}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B^{\prime}}}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all}\quad k<m-1,\quad k\equiv m-1\pmod{2}.

(2.34c)

Conditions (
2.34a
), (
2.34b
) and (
2.34c
) can equivalently be expressed as

\displaystyle\pi^{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\pi^{{{\mathbf{B}}}}_{{{a}}}=0,\qquad\omega^{{{a}{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.62248pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.62248pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.72275pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.50552pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\omega^{{{\mathbf{B^{\prime}}}}}_{{{a}}}=0,\qquad\pi^{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\omega^{{{\mathbf{B^{\prime}}}}}_{{{a}}}+2\omega^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B^{\prime}}}=0,

respectively. By Cartan’s theory of spinors, condition (
2.34a
) is equivalent to
\pi^{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}}
being pure provided it is non-zero, and similarly for conditions (
2.34b
) for
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
. Condition (
2.34c
) is equivalent to the
\alpha
-plane of
\pi^{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}}
and the
\beta
-plane of
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
intersecting in an
(m-1)
-plane in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
provided these are non-zero.

Just as in the odd-dimensional case, the twistor space of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m}
is obtained by removing the
\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)
-dimensional complex submanifold
\widehat{\infty}
corresponding to
\infty
on
\mathcal{Q}^{2m}
from
\mathbb{PT}
. We can use
[\pi^{\mathbf{A^{\prime}}}]
as homogeneous coordinates on the fibers of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}}
, and the incidence relation (
2.29
) can be expressed as
\omega^{\bm{A}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
.

2.4
Co-
\bm{\gamma}
-planes and mini-twistor space

In odd dimensions, there is an additional geometric object of interest.

Definition 2.23
.

A
co-
\gamma
-plane
is an
(m+1)
-dimensional affine subspace of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
with the property that the orthogonal complement of its tangent space at any of its point is totally null with respect to the metric.

The space of all co-
\gamma
-planes in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
is called the
mini-twistor space
of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
, and is denoted
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
.

Viewed as a vector subspace of
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}\cong\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
, a co-
\gamma
-plane through a point
x
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
is the orthogonal complement of a
\gamma
-plane through
x
. Consider a co-
\gamma
-plane through the origin, and let
[\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
be a projective pure spinor associated to the
\gamma
-plane orthogonal to it. Then, it is easy to check that this co-
\gamma
-plane consists of the set of points
x^{a}
satisfying
t\pi^{\mathbf{A}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
where
t\in\mathbb{C}
with
x^{a}x_{a}=-2t^{2}
. Shifting the origin to
\mathring{x}^{a}
say, a point in a co-
\gamma
-plane containing
\mathring{x}^{a}
now satisfies
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}+\pi^{\mathbf{A}}t=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
for some
t\in\mathbb{C}
, and where
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathring{x}^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
. Thus, a co-
\gamma
-plane through
\mathring{x}^{a}
consists of the set of points satisfying the incidence relation

\displaystyle\omega^{[\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}]}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\pi_{a}^{[\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}]},

(2.35)

where
[\pi^{\mathbf{C}}]
is a projective pure spinor and
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathring{x}^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
. In particular, a co-
\gamma
-plane consists of a
1
-parameter family of
\gamma
-planes, and thus corresponds to the curve

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\ni t\mapsto\big[\omega^{\mathbf{A}}+\pi^{\mathbf{A}}t,\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\big]\in\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}.

(2.36)

The relation between
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}
can be made precise by involving our choice of ‘infinity’
[\mathring{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}]
to define
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
. Let us write
(\mathring{Y}\cdot Z)^{\bm{\upalpha}}:=\mathring{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}Z^{\bm{\upbeta}}
. We can then define the vector field

\displaystyle\bm{Y}:=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\big(\mathring{Y}\cdot Z\big)^{\bm{\upalpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega^{\mathbf{A}}},

(2.37)

on
\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}
, the factors having been added for later convenience. It is now pretty clear that the curve (
2.36
) is an integral curve of the vector field (
2.37
) passing through the point
[\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
. We therefore conclude

Lemma 2.24
.

Mini-twistor space
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
is the quotient of
\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}
by the flow of
\bm{Y}
defined by (
2.37
).

An alternative geometric interpretation can be obtained by introducing weighted homogeneous coordinates on
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
as follows. Since
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
is pure, we can view
[\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
as homogeneous coordinates on
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
. Let
\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}
be an
(m-1)
-form satisfying

\displaystyle\pi^{a_{1}\mathbf{A}}\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}a_{2}\ldots a_{m-1}}=0,\qquad m>1.

(2.38)

Write
[\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]_{2,1}
for the equivalence class of pairs
(\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}})
defined by the relation

\displaystyle\big(\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\big)\sim\big(\lambda^{2}\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}},\lambda\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\big)\qquad\text{for some}\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}.

Then
[\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]_{2,1}
constitute weighted homogeneous coordinates on
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
. To see this, we note that for any choice of representative, the condition (
2.38
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}=\gamma^{{{(m-1)}}}_{{{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\omega^{\mathbf{B}}

(2.39)

for some pure spinor
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying (
2.31b
). Then, the projection of any
[\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
in
\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}
to
[\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]_{2,1}
is independent of the choice of representative of
[\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
, and further, since
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
is pure, i.e., satisfies (
2.31a
), sending
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
to
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}+t\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
for any
t\in\mathbb{C}
leaves (
2.39
) unchanged.

With these coordinates, we can rewrite the incidence relation (
2.35
) as

\displaystyle\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x^{a}\gamma^{{{(m)}}}_{{{aa_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}.

(2.40)

Now, turning to the geometrical interpretation, we fix a point
\pi
in
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
with a choice of pure spinor
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
. Since
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{\pi}\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
is a dense open subset of an
m
-dimensional linear subspace of
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
containing
\pi
, we can identify a vector in
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{\pi}\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
with a point in this subspace, which can be represented by a pure spinor
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying (
2.31b
). At this stage, this identification is valid provided the scale of
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
is
fixed
. Clearly the origin in
\mathrm{T}^{-1}_{\pi}\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
is
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
itself, so that
(\omega^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}})
maps injectively to
(\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}a_{2}\ldots a_{m-1}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}})
. That this map is also surjective follows immediately from (
2.39
). Hence, we can conclude

Proposition 2.25
.

The mini-twistor space
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
is a
\frac{1}{2}m(m+3)
-dimensional complex manifold isomorphic to the total space of the canonical rank-
m
distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
of the twistor space
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m-1}
.

For clarity, we represent
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
by means of an extended double fibration

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{PT}_{\setminus\widehat{\infty}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}}

where
\mu
,
\nu
,
\tau
and
\eta
are the usual projections. We shall introduce the following notation for submanifolds of
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
corresponding to points in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
:

\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{x}:=\nu^{-1}(x)\mapsto\underline{\hat{x}}:=\tau(\hat{x})=\eta(\mathbb{F}_{x}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{U}}:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{U}}\nu^{-1}(x)\mapsto\underline{\widehat{\mathcal{U}}}:=\tau\big(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\big)=\eta(\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{U}}).

Remark 2.26
.

For a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{F}
to descend to
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
, it must be annihilated by the differential operator
\pi^{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\nabla_{a}
.

2.5
Normal bundles

It will also be convenient to think of the correspondence space as an analytic family
\{\hat{x}\}
of compact complex submanifolds of twistor space parametrised by the points
x
of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
. The way each
\hat{x}
is embedded in
\mathbb{PT}
is described by its (holomorphic)
normal bundle

\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
in

\mathbb{PT}
, which is the rank-
(m+1)
vector bundle defined by the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\hat{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\left.\mathbb{PT}\right|_{\hat{x}}\rightarrow\mathrm{N}\hat{x}\rightarrow 0.

As we shall see there are some crucial difference between the odd- and even-dimensional cases.

2.5.1
Odd dimensions

Assume
n=2m+1
. We first note that the canonical distribution
\mathrm{D}
on
\mathbb{PT}
defines a subbundle
\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}+\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
of
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{PT}|_{\hat{x}}
containing
\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
. How much of this subbundle descends to
\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
is answered by the following lemma.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
x
be a point in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
. Then, for any
Z\in\hat{x}\subset\mathbb{PT}
, the intersection of
\mathrm{D}_{Z}
and
\mathrm{T}_{Z}\hat{x}
has dimension
m
. In particular,
\hat{x}
is equipped with a maximally non-integrable rank-
m
distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\hat{x}:=\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}\cap\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
. Further, there is a distinguished line subbundle of the normal bundle
\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
of
\hat{x}
given by
\mathrm{N}^{-1}\hat{x}:=(\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}+\mathrm{T}\hat{x})/\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
.

Proof.

Denote by
[X^{\mathcal{A}}]
the homogeneous coordinates of
x\in\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
, and let
\Xi\in\hat{x}\subset\mathbb{PT}
so that
X^{\mathcal{A}}\Xi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{\upalpha}}=0
. Then, by Lemma
2.7
and Proposition
2.8
, a vector tangent to
{\mathrm{D}}_{\Xi}
can be identified with a point
Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(Z_{(-1)}\cdot\Xi)^{\bm{\upalpha}}
of a dense open subset of
\mathbf{D}_{\Xi}\subset\mathbb{PT}
. Here,
Z_{(-1)}\in\mathfrak{r}_{-1}\cong\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
lies in a complement of the stabiliser
\mathfrak{r}
of
\Xi
as explained in Section
2.2
. The condition that this vector is also tangent to
\hat{x}
is equivalent to
0=X^{\mathcal{A}}Z^{{{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}=-2X_{\mathcal{A}}Z^{\mathcal{A}}_{(-1)}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
, by (
2.1
), i.e.,
X_{\mathcal{A}}Z^{\mathcal{A}}_{(-1)}=0
. This gives a single additional algebraic condition on
Z_{(-1)}^{\mathcal{A}}
, and thus the intersection of
\mathrm{D}_{\Xi}
and
\mathrm{T}_{\Xi}\hat{x}
is
m
-dimensional (for a description in affine coordinates, see the end of Appendix
A.1
). This defines a rank-
m
distribution
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\hat{x}:=\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}\cap\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
on
\hat{x}
. Since
\mathrm{D}
is maximally non-integrable, so must be
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\hat{x}
. That the subbundle
\mathrm{N}^{-1}\hat{x}:=(\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}+\mathrm{T}\hat{x})/\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
of
\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
is of rank
1
follows from the isomorphism
\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}/(\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}\cap\mathrm{T}\hat{x})\cong(\mathrm{D}|_{\hat{x}}+\mathrm{T}\hat{x})/\mathrm{T}\hat{x}
.
∎

That
\hat{x}
is endowed with a canonical rank-
m
distribution comes as no surprise since each
\hat{x}
is isomorphic to the generalised flag manifold
P/Q\cong\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-1)}
.

As explained in
[
24
]
, the tangent space at a point
x
of
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
injects into
H^{0}(\hat{x},\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{N}\hat{x}))
, the space of global holomorphic sections of
\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
. If
V^{a}
is a vector in
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
and
y
the point infinitesimally separated from
x
by
V^{a}
, then the corresponding section of
H^{0}(\hat{x},\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{N}\hat{x}))
can be identified with
\hat{y}
. Let us fix
x
to be the origin in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
. Then
V^{a}
can be identified with
y^{a}
. We view
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
as coordinates on
\hat{x}
given by the locus
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}=0
. The infinitesimal displacement of
\hat{x}
along
V^{a}
at the origin is
V^{\mathbf{A}}:=V^{a}\nabla_{a}\omega^{\mathbf{A}}
, i.e.,
V^{\mathbf{A}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}V^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
. This represents a global holomorphic section
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
of
\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
, and can be identified with the complex submanifold
\hat{y}
given by
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}y^{a}\pi_{a}^{\mathbf{A}}
.

Before describing such sections, we shall need the following two lemmata.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
V^{a}
be a non-zero vector in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
, and let
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}:=V^{a}\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mathbf{B}}}}_{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00844pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.86317pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
be the corresponding spin endomorphism. Then
V^{a}
is null if and only if
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
has a zero eigenvalue. Further,

•

if
V^{a}
is null,
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
has a single zero eigenvalue of algebraic multiplicity
2^{m}
, and its eigenspace is isomorphic to the
2^{m-1}
-dimensional spinor space of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m-1}
,

•

if
V^{a}
is non-null,
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
has a pair of eigenvalues
\pm\mathrm{i}\sqrt{V^{a}V_{a}}
, each of algebraic multiplicity
2^{m-1}
, and their respective eigenspaces are isomorphic to the
2^{m-1}
-dimensional chiral spinor spaces of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m}
.

Proof.

By the Clifford property, we have
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{C}}V_{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathbf{B}}=-V^{a}V_{a}\delta_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
, and it follows that any eigenvalue of
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
must be equal to
\pm\mathrm{i}\sqrt{V^{a}V_{a}}
. Hence
V^{a}
is null if and only if it has a zero eigenvalue. This zero eigenvalue must be of algebraic multiplicity
2^{m}
since in this case
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
is nilpotent. One can check that the kernel of
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
can be identified with the
2^{m-1}
-dimensional spinor space of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m-1}
as the orthogonal complement of
V^{a}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
quotiented by
\langle V^{a}\rangle
.

If
V^{a}
is non-null, the square of
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
is proportional to the identity, and thus, each of the eigenvalues
\pm\mathrm{i}\sqrt{V^{a}V_{a}}
must have algebraic multiplicity
2^{m-1}
. Each of the eigenspaces can be identified with each of the chiral spinor spaces of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m}
as the orthogonal complement of
\langle V^{a}\rangle
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
– see, e.g.,
[
31
]
.
∎

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
x
and
y
be two points in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
infinitesimally separated by a non-null vector
V^{a}
. Then, for every
Z\in\hat{x}\subset\mathbb{PT}
such that
V^{a}
is tangent to the co-
\gamma
-plane
\check{Z}^{\perp}\subset\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
,
\mathrm{D}_{Z}
intersects
\hat{y}
in a unique point
W
, say, such that the corresponding
\gamma
-planes
\check{Z}
and
\check{W}
intersect maximally.

Proof.

With no loss of generality, we may assume that
x
is the origin in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
. We then have
V^{a}=y^{a}
. Since
V^{a}
is non-null, it must lie on some co-
\gamma
-plane of some twistor
Z
. Following the discussion of Section
2.4
, it can be represented by a
1
-parameter family of
\gamma
-planes. In particular,
y
must lie on one such
\gamma
-plane. If
Z
is a point on
\hat{x}
, then
[Z^{\bm{\upalpha}}]=[0,\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
for some
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
. The condition that
y
lies on the co-
\gamma
-plane
Z^{\perp}
is that
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
is an eigenspinor of
V^{a}
with eigenvalue
t
or
-t
where
t:=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{V^{a}V_{a}}
. For definiteness, let us assume that the eigenvalue is
t
. With reference to (
2.36
), the point
y^{a}
lies in the
\gamma
-plane
W
given by
[W^{\bm{\upalpha}}]=[t\pi^{\mathbf{A}},\pi^{\mathbf{A}}]
. Re-expressing this twistor as
W^{\bm{\upalpha}}=\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}-\frac{t}{2}\mathring{Y}^{\mathcal{A}}\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.90282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\bm{\upalpha}}}}_{{{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\upbeta}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\Xi^{\bm{\upalpha}}
, we see, by Lemma
2.7
, that
W
lies in the intersection of
\mathrm{D}_{Z}
and
\hat{y}
. In fact, one can see that the connecting vector from
Z
to
W
is given by
\sqrt{V^{a}V_{a}}\bm{Y}
, where
\bm{Y}
is given by (
2.37
). Finally, by Proposition
2.12
,
Z
and
W
must intersect maximally.
∎

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
x
be a point in
\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
with corresponding submanifold
\hat{x}
in
\mathbb{PT}
. Let
V
be a tangent vector at
x
, and
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
its corresponding global holomorphic section of
\mathrm{N}\hat{x}
.

•

Suppose
V
is null. When
m=1
,
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
vanishes at a single point on
\hat{x}
, which corresponds to the unique
\gamma
-plane
(
i.e., null line
)
to which
V
is tangent. When
m>1
, there is a
\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)
-dimensional algebraic subset of
\hat{x}
biholomorphic to
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-3)}
on which
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
vanishes. Each point of this subset corresponds to a
\gamma
-plane to which
V
is tangent.

•

Suppose
V
is non-null. When
m=1
, there are precisely two points,
Z_{\pm}
say, on
\hat{x}
, at which
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}(Z_{\pm})\in\mathrm{N}^{-1}_{Z_{\pm}}\hat{x}
. Further,
V
is tangent to the two co-
\gamma
-planes determined by
Z_{\pm}
. When
m>1
, there are two disjoint
\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)
-dimensional algebraic subsets of
\hat{x}
, biholomorphic to
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-2)}
and
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}_{(2m-2)}
, over which
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
is a section of
\mathrm{N}^{-1}\hat{x}
. Each point of these subsets corresponds to a co-
\gamma
-plane to which
V
is tangent.

Conversely, if
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
vanishes at a point, then
V
must be null, and if
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}(Z)\in\mathrm{N}^{-1}_{Z}\hat{x}
for some
Z\in\hat{x}
, then
V
must be non-null.

Proof.

Again, let us assume that
x
is the origin in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{2m+1}
, and set
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}:=V^{a}\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.775pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.26025pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.04303pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mathbf{B}}}}_{{{a}{\mathbf{A}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.39859pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00844pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.86317pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
.

If
V^{a}
is null, the vanishing of
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
of a point
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
of
\hat{x}
is simply equivalent to
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}=0
, i.e.,
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
is a pure eigenspinor of
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
. By Lemma
2.28
, we can immediately conclude that
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
vanishes at a point when
m=1
, and on a subset of
\hat{x}
biholomorphic to
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-3)}
when
m>1
. Clearly, each point of this subset corresponds to a
\gamma
-plane to which
V^{a}
is tangent.

If
V^{a}
is non-null, we know by Lemma
2.28
that
V_{\mathbf{A}}^{\mathbf{B}}
has eigenvalues
\pm\mathrm{i}\sqrt{V^{a}V_{a}}
. In particular, the pure eigenspinors up to scale determine two distinct points on
\hat{x}
when
m=1
, and two disjoint subsets of
\hat{x}
biholomorphic to the twistor spaces
\mathbb{PT}_{(2m-2)}
and
\mathbb{PT}^{\prime}_{(2m-2)}
when
m>1
. A point
Z
on any of these sets corresponds to a co-
\gamma
-plane
\check{Z}^{\perp}
to which
V^{a}
is tangent. By Lemma
2.29
, the corresponding submanifold
\hat{y}
intersects
\mathrm{D}_{Z}
at a point
W
. The connecting vector from
Z
to
W
clearly lies in
\mathrm{D}_{Z}
, but is not tangent to
\hat{x}
. In particular, it descends to an element of
\mathrm{N}_{Z}^{-1}\hat{x}
. Thus, the restriction of
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
to these subsets is a section of
\mathrm{N}^{-1}\hat{x}
.

Finally, if
\widehat{V}_{\hat{x}}
vanishes at a point
Z
say, then
V
is tangent to the
\gamma
-plane
\check{Z}
, and so must be null. The non-null case is similar.
∎

2.5.2
Mini-twistor space

For any point
x
of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
, the normal bundle
\mathrm{N}\underline{\hat{x}}
of
\underline{\hat{x}}
in
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
is given by
0{\rightarrow}\mathrm{T}\underline{\hat{x}}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{T}\left.\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}\right|_{\underline{\hat{x}}}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{N}\underline{\hat{x}}{\rightarrow}0
. In this case,
\mathrm{N}\underline{\hat{x}}
can be identified with
\mathrm{T}^{-1}\hat{x}
, i.e., mini-twistor space itself, as follows from the description of Section
2.4
: taking
x
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
to be the origin, then the complex submanifold
\underline{\hat{x}}
in
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
is defined by
\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}=0
,
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
will be coordinates on
\underline{\hat{x}}
, and we shall view
\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}
as coordinates off
\underline{\hat{x}}
.

Again, for any
x\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
,
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
injects into
H^{0}(\underline{\hat{x}},\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{N}\underline{\hat{x}}))
. If
x
is the origin and
V\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{n}
be the vector connecting
x
to a point
y
, we can identify the global holomorphic section
\widehat{V}_{\underline{\hat{x}}}
of
\mathrm{N}\underline{\hat{x}}
as in the previous section. If
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}
are coordinates on
\hat{x}
given by the locus
\omega^{\mathbf{A}}=0
,
\widehat{V}_{\underline{\hat{x}}}
can be identified with the complex submanifold
\underline{\hat{y}}
given by
\underline{\omega}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}y^{a}\gamma^{{{(m)}}}_{{{aa_{1}\ldots a_{m-1}}{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}}}}\pi^{\mathbf{A}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}
, where
y^{a}=V^{a}
.

Proposition 2.31
.

Let
x
be a point in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{E}^{2m+1}
with corresponding submanifold
\underline{\hat{x}}
in
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{T}
. Let
V
be a tangent vector at
x
